That is why an own start target must lie inside the install folder: what runs from there CouchHUD recognises as this game and counts its time. An entry without an install folder (web entry, Store app) gets no own start target. For a game the rule of adding applies: no shell, no interpreter, no browser, nothing from the Windows folder, also when an app is switched to a game later.
- **A batch file in the main folder (since 0.7.0):** when a game keeps its exe in a program folder below its main folder (Call of Duty: `Call of Duty\_retail_\cod.exe`, Unreal: `Binaries\Win64`, `bin\x64`), a found entry's install folder is that program folder. A `.bat`/`.cmd` next to it in the main folder was refused in 0.6.3 as "outside the game's folder". For a batch file (and its working folder) the main folder now counts as well (`LaunchOverrides.ScriptRoot`): only through fixed names of such program folders (`_retail_`, `bin`, `Binaries`, `Win64`, `x64` …, three levels at most), never up to a drive, the Windows folder, the program folders or the user profile (`GameSessionTracker.SaneRoot`). Only through program-folder names, never above the game's own folder, never a library folder: walking up stops as soon as the parent folder is or contains a watched game folder (the "game folders" setting), contains another entry's install folder, is or holds `steamapps` or `steamapps\common`, or is `C:\Users`, `C:\ProgramData` or `C:\Users\Public` (`LaunchFileSystem.LibraryFolders`, `GameSessionTracker.SaneRoot`). So `D:\Games\bin\game.exe` stays at `bin` and `D:\Games` is not reached while it is watched; the stop only knows watched folders, known entries and the Steam layout, so a lone unwatched library can still be walked up to (behind the parents' code for the change); a game folder with an ordinary name does not open upwards anyway. When the main folder itself is a link it is checked too before a working folder below it counts. An `.exe` stays inside the install folder (detection and play time go by it); cmd.exe is not part of the game's process chain anyway, so the play time does not change. Content (SHA-256), the check for redirects below the folder, the working-folder rule and cmd.exe's quoting rules are unchanged; a file inside the install folder is checked from there as before. The refusal now names the folder the file belongs in. When the host wants the parents' code for the change, the page asks for it and makes the change once more (before, only "The parent code is required" showed when the page had not loaded the parental status yet).
- **Path:** stored in its canonical form (`Path.GetFullPath`), only on a local drive (`LocalPaths.IsAllowed`: no UNC or network paths, no `\\?\` or `\??\` prefixes, no streams, no mapped network drives), no device names (CON, NUL, COM1 … also as a folder), only `.exe`, `.bat`, `.cmd`, at most 260 characters, no control characters, and the file itself is no redirect (`SavePathGuard.IsRedirect`). At the start the target is checked again the same way (exists, inside the folder, local, no redirect, canonical); when it no longer passes, nothing starts instead of something else.
- **Batch files:** a `.bat`/`.cmd` starts as `System32\cmd.exe /d /v:off /s /c ""<file>" <parameters>"` directly (CreateProcess, no ShellExecute: the lock forbids `cmd.exe` through the shell, and no file handler reads the line). `/d` turns AutoRun off, `/v:off` delayed expansion. cmd.exe has its own quoting rules, and a batch file can read its parameters a second time with `call`; so CouchHUD refuses `& | ^ < > ( ) % !` and quotes inside a value in a batch file's parameters instead of escaping them (no escaping is safe for every script). In the path `%`, `!` and `"` are refused (the path is always quoted, where the other characters are text; "Program Files (x86)" works). The check applies when saving and at the start, also for own entries that start a batch file. An `.exe` starts as before without shell parsing (each parameter on its own by the rules of `CommandLineToArgvW`).
- **Without a console window:** cmd.exe starts with `CREATE_NO_WINDOW` (`LaunchOptions.StartInfo`, no shell), so no console window shows in the taskbar. Holding, checksum, quoting, parameters and working folder are unchanged; nothing is redirected (standard input stays, otherwise `timeout` stops). Input in the script (`pause`, `set /p`, `choice`) could not be seen or answered by anyone, and Tasks, Quit and `SafeTermination` do not touch a Windows program such as cmd.exe. So the launcher watches exactly the cmd.exe it started (process id and start time) every 5 s (`ScriptWatch`, `ScriptRun`). Working child processes are for example a game started in the foreground, `timeout`, `ping` or `tasklist`; `conhost.exe` and `choice.exe` from the Windows folder do not count, a child counts only when it was created after the cmd.exe, and one that cannot be read counts as working. It is ended only after 3 checks in a row without a working child (about 15 s; a single empty sample, say between two tools of a waiting loop, never ends it): from 30 s of run time on, or earlier when its game session is over. Only a session that began after the script counts as its session; when a new session of the entry begins, it counts as running again. While anything runs under it, it stays after the game too. When CouchHUD quits, every watched cmd.exe without a working child is ended (it would otherwise go on waiting unwatched). It is always ended with `Win32ProcessControl.Kill(pid, start)`: the start time is compared on the handle that ends it; never by name, never another cmd.exe, never its children. A notice says "Start script of … was waiting for input – ended". The file's hold ends with the cmd.exe. A script that does something built in for longer without a child process (a loop with `goto`) is ended as well; start scripts should not ask for anything.
- **A batch file's content:** the approval covers the content, not only the path. When a `.bat`/`.cmd` is picked as the start target, CouchHUD keeps the file's size and SHA-256 (`LaunchTargetSha256`, `LaunchTargetSize`). At the start the file is opened with `FileShare.Read` and compared on exactly that handle; the handle stays open while cmd.exe runs, so nobody changes, renames or deletes it between the check and the read or during the run. When it differs, nothing starts and the hint says plainly "was changed since it was approved – parents must confirm the start target again": parents confirm by picking the file again under Edit › Startup options › Start target (with the unlock and the parents' code). A change of only the parameters or the working folder does not confirm a changed file. A batch file over 1 MB is neither approved nor started as a start target (`FileFingerprint.MaxScriptBytes`; a start script is a few lines, and so the check holds up no start), and the hint says so. A batch file without a kept content (older overrides, own entries and found entries that start a batch file) starts only on an open couch, never on a locked one.
- **Folders without a detour:** below the install folder no redirect (junction, symbolic link; `SavePathGuard.HasReparsePointBelow`) may lie on the way to the start target and the working folder, checked when saving and at every start; a folder link put in afterwards stops the start. The install folder itself may be a link (moved libraries). For a batch file the working folder must lie inside the install folder (otherwise cmd.exe reads files from elsewhere), and its parameters are plain ASCII only: Windows' "best fit" turns lookalikes such as a full-width ＆ into the real character when converting to the code page.
- **Grants only after success:** the parents' code for `startOptions` is used up only once the change is checked and saved, the one for `launch` only after the start; a refused change or start keeps the grant. Which client is started first and which windows may come to the front follows the target that is really started.
- **For every way:** overrides apply to every start through `Hud.Launch`, so also to starts through MCP and the assistant (`HudTools`); they start the same checked target, they cannot change it.
- **Limits:** parameters at most 2000 characters, no control characters (as `LaunchOptions`), a working folder must exist and be local.
- **Steam and launchers:** CouchHUD writes nothing into Steam's files (`localconfig.vdf` etc.) and touches no accounts. `steam://run/<AppID>//<parameters>/` does not pass parameters reliably (reports of them being ignored, and Steam may ask about unknown parameters); so CouchHUD sets no parameters for Steam games but says where they are in Steam. Epic and Ubisoft Connect start through their launcher link, which takes no parameters; that is said just as plainly. An own start target in the game's folder works for all of them.
- Known limits: an own start target is as powerful as any program of the user; a batch file in the game's folder can start programs elsewhere. The approval covers only the chosen batch file, not the scripts or programs it calls. CouchHUD does not recognise those as this game (cmd.exe is a Windows program and not part of the game's process chain), so their time does not count. That is why the change sits behind the unlock and the parents' code. Whoever can write `config.json` as the same user can change the override; the check at the start still holds folder, drive and file type. An `.exe` start target is not pinned by its content (game updates replace it); whoever can swap an exe in the game's folder could just as well swap the game's found exe. Whoever can write `config.json` can also set a batch file's checksum and size anew.

### Store match and own texts

Threat model since 0.5.0 ("Choose a store entry" and an own description, developer, publisher, release date, genres). The store match decides the age rating parental controls block games by (`AgeOf` through the store entry). Attacker: a child who matches a game rated 18 to a harmless store entry (or "no store entry") to get past the age limit; also the phone remote, MCP, plugins and the assistant; the store's answers as untrusted data.
- **Who may match:** Only the HUD's own page through the bridge (`searchStore`, `getItemInfo`, `setStoreMatch`, `setItemInfo`), never on a locked couch (not in `LockPolicy.OpenWhileLocked`), never while a protected profile waits at the picker (`RequireProfileChosen`). With parental controls every change of the match, "automatic again" included, needs the parents' code for exactly this entry (action `storeMatch`). The assistant's and MCP's `edit_item` knows neither the match nor the texts, plugins and the phone remote have no way there (contract tests `StoreMatchContractTests`). Steam games keep their own app id; there is no choice for them.
- **The stricter rating until confirmed:** When the match changes without the parents' code (parental controls off, or no code set), CouchHUD keeps the rating so far as a floor; the stricter of the old and the new match counts, "no store entry" included, and a further change does not lower it. Only a change with the parents' code ("Confirm the rating") lifts the floor. A cached answer counts only for the match it was fetched for (its source is in the cache); after a change the rating is looked up again instead of taking over the old one.
- **Renaming changes nothing:** The automatic match goes by the name the entry was found with, not by the one the user gave it. Before, a rename (without the parents' code) could bring another store entry, and with it another rating, once the cache ran out.
- **Own texts:** description at most 1000 characters, release date 40, developers/publishers/genres at most 8 entries of 80 characters; no control characters and no invisible formatting characters (direction changes, zero width). The page sets them as text (`textContent`), never as HTML. They only change what is shown; an own age rating does not exist on purpose. Values from a hand-edited `config.json` are bounded again when shown.
- **Network:** The search uses the same Steam endpoint as the automatic match (`store.steampowered.com/api/storesearch`) through the existing polite client (`PoliteHandler`, `SafeNet`: public addresses only, rate limit), a query of at most 100 characters without control characters, a 15 second time limit, an answer of at most 512 KB, at most 10 hits (games only, no DLC), names bounded and cleaned; store details at most 4 MB. Only a checked app id (digits) or "none" is stored. No new network target; SteamGridDB gives no store data and still only serves pictures.
- **Automatic match:** none rather than a wrong one. Names in another script (Japanese, Cyrillic …) are not compared, two different years in brackets are different games, a number alone is not enough, and when several different store entries fit, none counts unless exactly one has the very name.
- **A rating vanishing from the automatic match:** when the automatic lookup by name later gives a game whose rating was known none or a milder one (the store entry became ambiguous, disappeared or was rated differently), the rating so far stays as a floor (`StoreAgeFloor`) until parents confirm the match with the parents' code. When the answer is missing altogether (offline, error), the old one stays in the cache. Steam games with their own app id and entries picked by hand are left out (the rule above applies there).
- **Old rating unknown:** when the rating so far is not known at a change of the match (never looked up, offline), there is no floor; with "Block unknown ratings" off the game then needs no code.
- Known limits: whoever can write `config.json` as the same user can change the match and the floor (like any setting). A new match without the parents' code keeps as its floor the strictest rating so far over every system and Steam's own (`AgeRatings.KeptAge`), not only the active system's: switching the region and then matching something harmless frees no game. The rating comes from the Steam store (system by region, see "Age ratings by region"); if the chosen entry has none, it counts as unknown (blocked with "Block unknown ratings").

### Screenshots with the controller

Threat model since 0.5.0 (hold Guide + Y, *Screenshot* in the Guide menu). A screenshot can show whatever is on screen, codes and keys too.
- **Who triggers it:** Only the real controller (the combination checks that Guide is really pressed on the pad; buttons from the phone do not come this way) and the entry in the Guide menu of CouchHUD's own page (`capture`). The phone can drive the Guide menu but cannot take a screenshot: when the last button came from the phone (and not later from the controller, the PC's keyboard or mouse), the host refuses the menu entry; the phone never gets to see a picture anyway. No new operation to the service, no MCP tool, no plugin or assistant interface. At most one per second, never two at once.
- **When not:** While a code entry is shown (the page reports it through `codeEntryState`, also on a locked couch and for a waiting profile; besides, the host itself waits for the Windows sign-in or the parents' code) or the on-screen keyboard is open on a secret or pasted field or the phone's pairing code is shown (QR code or the code as text, `pairingState`), CouchHUD refuses with a message. When the page reloads or its renderer crashes, the host forgets these reports and refuses until the new page has reported both states; the page repeats its report until the host took it, and every few seconds after. Right before the capture it checks again; when a code entry or the pairing code shows up during the capture, the picture is thrown away. The menu entry checks again after its delay. At most 20 screenshots a minute (only ones taken count), and none when less than 1 GB is free on the drive or the free space is unknown (each with a message). Not on the couch browser's pages and not in mouse mode (launcher sign-ins). Under parental controls a child may take screenshots. The whole screen is captured: notifications from Windows and other programs that are showing right then (a chat, a sign-in code in a toast) end up in the picture; only CouchHUD's own messages are closed first.
- **Where to:** Only `%USERPROFILE%\Pictures\CouchHUD\Screenshots` (on purpose not the Pictures known folder, which often lives in OneDrive), nothing is uploaded. A folder link or junction below the profile (`Pictures`, `CouchHUD`, `Screenshots`) stops the save, checked before and after creating the folders; the file is always created new (`CreateNew`) and its real path checked on the open handle. Until it is fully written it is marked delete-on-close on that handle: when the check or the write fails it goes away with the handle, without opening a name again. CouchHUD's own messages (the screenshot message in CouchHUD, the small window over a game) are closed before the capture. The name from the game's name is cleaned (letters, digits and a few signs only; no path or control characters, no device names such as `CON` or `COM1`, at most 60 characters). Only the file name goes to the log.
- **Who sees them:** The gallery of CouchHUD's own page (through `local.couchhud`, as before for Game Bar, Steam and NVIDIA). The phone remote, MCP, plugins and the assistant have no read access to captures. The preview in the message loads from `local.couchhud` only.
- Known limits: whoever is signed in as the same user can read the folder and capture the screen anyway. The game sees the Y press too. Secrets that do not go through a code entry or a secret field (such as the MCP key in the settings, a game's web page) are not covered by the refusal. When the Game Bar or Steam owns Guide, their overlay may end up in the picture. The "keyboard or mouse" origin is reported by the page itself; whoever controls the page could do more anyway.

### The couch browser and Windows dialogs (from 0.7.0)

Threat model: a child at the couch browser (code lock, parental controls, shell mode, standard account). The browser must never lead to a Windows dialog (open, save, print, color picker): from there right-click, "Open with" and the address bar lead to Explorer and cmd. Several layers, from independent of the page inwards:
- **Web view settings:** no default context menu (and `ContextMenuRequested` handled as well), no browser accelerator keys (Ctrl+P, Ctrl+S), no script dialogs, no host objects, no dropping files from Windows (`AllowExternalDrop` off), downloads through `DownloadStarting` with `Handled` (locked: cancelled; otherwise the HUD asks, the browser never shows its own window).
- **File chooser intercepted:** through the DevTools protocol (`Page.setInterceptFileChooserDialog`) a page's file chooser (also from a shadow DOM) makes no Windows dialog at all. This holds for the top-level page; frames in a process of their own (another site) are not covered by it.
- **Script guard in every document and frame:** `composedPath()` instead of `event.target` (shadow DOM fields are recognised), `attachShadow` is patched (closed shadow roots get the guard too), `print`, `showOpenFilePicker`, `showSaveFilePicker`, `showDirectoryPicker`, `input.click()`/`showPicker()` for file and color fields, dropping files. WebView2 runs the script on every navigation of the page and its frames (`AddScriptToExecuteOnDocumentCreated`), but the initial empty document of a frame a script makes (`about:blank`, `srcdoc`) is no navigation: so the script patches frames on the first read of `contentWindow`/`contentDocument`, watches new frames and loads, and the host also runs it at each frame's `ContentLoading`. This is the first layer only.
- **Native backstop (`BrowserDialogGuard`):** while the couch lock is on (also during the short unlock for the browser), CouchHUD is the shell, not in PC mode and not with the developer switch, a WinEvent hook (dialog start, window created or shown, out of context) closes every standard dialog (window class `#32770`: open, save, print, color, the common item dialog) that belongs to the process tree of the couch browser's web view the moment it appears, plus a pass over the windows every 500 ms for missed events. The match is by the process id of the web view's browser process (`BrowserProcessId`) and its descendants with a start-time check, not by name alone. Not touched: the HUD interface's own web view and games (the foreground guard keeps dealing with those). Fail-closed: when the process tree cannot be told, the rule "Unclear match" below applies; a dialog that cannot be closed after about 1.2 s ends the couch browser (and the dialog with it); when the hook is not running, the couch browser does not open at all under these rules.
- **PDF viewer, certificate dialog, screen sharing:** the built-in PDF viewer's toolbar hides Print, Save and Save as (`HiddenPdfToolbarItems`; the `IsPdfToolbarEnabled` switch is missing in this SDK version). When a page asks for a client certificate (`ClientCertificateRequested`) or a screen share (`ScreenCaptureStarting`), both are refused without a window.
- **Structural rule (not only dialogs):** every other **visible top-level window** in the couch browser's process tree is closed as well, unless it belongs to the HUD's window tree (owner chain, `GA_ROOTOWNER`, leads to the HUD window: the pane's select lists and popups) or is a known helper window (input methods, tooltips, message windows). Hidden windows and child windows are never touched. The rule is a pure function with tests (`BrowserDialogRules.Decide`). A possible false positive (a browser popup without an owner) would cost convenience, not security.
- **Unclear match:** when a window cannot be matched to the process tree, it is closed only if it belongs to a WebView2 process **and** its owner chain leads to the HUD window. Dialogs of **other** WebView2 applications are left alone. When CouchHUD does not know the web view's process id (after a start phase of about 8 s), the couch browser is not run blind but ended, or not opened at all.
- **Hard finish:** before the couch browser is closed, CouchHUD notes the browser process's id and start time. If exactly that process is still alive about 1 s after the dispose, it is ended; the start time is compared on the handle (no reused id), never another process.
- **Limits, honestly:** the guard is reactive: a dialog can be visible for a fraction of a second before it is closed (it takes no controller input meanwhile, but a fast keyboard could hit it). Windows that are no standard dialogs (own window classes, such as the share, payment or sign-in windows) are not caught by the class list; only the script locks and the command line meet them. A dialog of another program in front (Steam, a game) falls under the foreground guard (`LockedForeground`, a poll with documented exceptions). The script cannot reach a frame it has no access to (another site, a closed shadow DOM made by the parser, `<template shadowrootmode="closed">`); there the file chooser interception and the native backstop carry. Whoever already runs code as the same user can end the hook anyway.

### The couch browser's history

With parental controls, switching *Remember recently visited sites* off needs the parents' code for exactly this action (`browserHistoryOff`), checked in the host and used up only after the save worked; switching it on is free. Off also deletes the history and the download list in the built-in browser's WebView2 profile, at the latest when it next opens (it is tried already when switching and when closing; a failure goes to the log). Until then the entries stay in the browser profile on disk, readable by the same Windows user.

### Switchable areas

Since 0.5.0 the areas Games, Apps, Browser and News can be switched off (*Settings › HUD › Areas*). Attacker: a child switching an area on or off to get around a gate (getting the browser back, say, or taking games out of the count); also the remote, MCP, plugins and the assistant.
- **Who may switch:** only the HUD's own page (`setFeatureAreas`), never on a locked couch (the method checks the lock itself and is not in `LockPolicy.OpenWhileLocked`), never while a protected profile waits at the picker, with parental controls only with the parents' code (action `areas`, checked before the change). The assistant and MCP know neither the call nor a setting key for it; plugins and the remote have no own path (tests `FeatureAreasTests`).
- **Off means refused:** bridge calls, settings and tools of an area that is off are refused in the host, not only hidden on the page; entries of an area that is off can neither be started nor added. Without the browser no entry that opens a web page or a browser program starts either, and the browser window is closed.
- **Store entries marked as apps:** a store with at least one entry typed as app by an override is read even with games off (`FeatureAreas.ReadsStore`); only those app entries are shown and can be started (`AreasOf`), its games stay refused.
- **Switching off loosens nothing:** with parental controls on, the game stores are read even with games off, play time and limits still apply to every game. What the foreground guard does on a locked couch (minimizing browsers and other programs) does not depend on the areas.
- **Saved like any setting:** with broken settings (`config.json` unreadable, also when the list is not an array) CouchHUD refuses the change before a parents' code is used up, and runs locked and strict anyway. The parents' code is used up only after the save worked. Unknown names in the list are ignored; a list that leaves none of games, apps and browser on (only possible by hand) counts as "everything on", in the host as on the page.
- **Game clients:** when Games goes off, CouchHUD closes only the clients it started in the background itself, under the same rules as on quit (Steam only, never during a download or a game). Clients that ran before or were started by the user, and other clients (Epic, GOG …), keep running.
- Known limits: whoever can write `config.json` as the same user can change the areas (like any setting). Whether an entry starts a browser is judged by the program's name: a shortcut (`.lnk`) or start script that starts a browser is judged by its own name, not by its target. Only an unlocked couch adds entries; on a locked couch the foreground guard minimizes browsers anyway.
### Age ratings by region (USK, PEGI, ESRB)

Threat model since 0.5.0. The age limit goes by the system of the Windows region or a fixed choice (USK, PEGI, ESRB); when a game has no rating there, the strictest of the other systems counts (ClassInd and CERO too), then Steam's `required_age`, else "unknown". Attackers: a child picking a milder system or changing the region; hostile or broken store answers; a hand-edited `config.json`.
- **Store data:** a rating that is there but cannot be read (no object, no number, an unknown code, more than three digits) counts as unreadable and never as mild: if it is the active system's, the age is unknown; if another one is unreadable while the active system's is missing, as well (it could have been the strictest). Then the rule for games without an age rating applies. Values above 21 are cut to 21. ESRB "RP" (rating pending) counts as no rating, the chain goes on. Older cache entries (one number only) keep counting with that number. The floor for a rating that went away compares with the strictest rating of all systems. When the active system's rating is missing, Steam's `required_age` counts too when it is stricter than the other systems.
- **Switching the system:** only from the HUD's own page (`setParental` with `ratingSystem`), never on a locked couch (`RequireUnlocked`), never with a broken `config.json` (`RefuseWhileConfigFaulted` before anything else). With parental controls and a parents' code it needs the code for exactly this action (`ratingSystem`), checked before the save and used up only after the save succeeded. A switch moves every age limit of every profile to the new system's nearest step at or below the old one, never looser (below the lowest step the old one stays). A child can change the Windows region (HKCU) without admin rights. So when the system or an age limit is saved, CouchHUD stores which system counted then (`AgeRatingSystemResolved`). When under "Automatic" the region later names another system, every game is judged by both and the stricter verdict wins (`AgeRatings.JudgedSystems`, `VerdictAge`) until the parents save the limit or the system again; a hint in parental controls names both systems as soon as any profile has an age limit. Setting a limit to "off" saves no system. A saved value edited by hand is read ignoring case and spaces; when it names no system, the strictest rating of all systems counts as well. The migration to "usk" checks exactly the key `ageRatingSystem` that loading reads too. A region change therefore never makes a blocked game allowed (a test over all systems, steps and sample games). The ESRB has no AO step: a stored 18 counts as M there (`LimitFor`), AO games stay blocked at every step. An existing `config.json` with an age limit and no `ageRatingSystem` gets "usk" once, as it counted before.
- **Hand-edited `config.json`:** an unknown system counts as "strictest rating", an age limit that is no step as the strictest step (3). Profiles with such a limit count as broken as before (default rules). An unreadable file leads into the locked stand-in as before.
- Known limits: the ratings come from the Steam store; when a game has none there, only the rule for games without an age rating helps. Whoever can write `config.json` as the same user can change the system and the limit (like any setting).

### Freezing the game and anti-cheat

Threat model since 0.5.0. "Pause game while CouchHUD is in front" (expert mode, off by default) and the parental pause when time is up suspend the game's processes with `NtSuspendProcess`. To an anti-cheat system that can look like tampering and get the player's account banned. So the danger is harm to the user, not an attacker.
- **Rule:** only a game whose check ran to the end and found nothing is frozen. Found (known names of Easy Anti-Cheat, BattlEye, Vanguard, nProtect GameGuard, XIGNCODE, PunkBuster, Ricochet, mhyprot, ACE, EQU8, FACEIT and others) or not checkable: no freeze, one honest message per game session. For the parental pause that means: the game keeps running, CouchHUD stays in front of it, the reminder every 20 seconds and the close after the grace time stay the same; play time keeps counting meanwhile.
- **Local evidence only, no network:** the names of files and folders in the game's install folder and the names and image paths of its running processes (the same ones play time counts). Only names are read, no contents.
- **Bounds of the scan:** at most 4 folder levels below the install folder, 50,000 names, 2 seconds, in the background when the game starts. Never into a junction or link (nothing outside the folder is read); when the install folder is itself a link or a drive, lies on a share (`\\server\…`, `\\?\UNC\…`) or a mapped network drive (then it is not touched at all), is missing, unreadable or a bound is hit, it counts as "not checkable". The same while the scan is still running at the moment of the freeze; that gets its own message ("still checking"), the real one still comes once later. A second game that ends forgets its scan like the main game.
- Known limits: an anti-cheat whose names CouchHUD does not know, or one outside the install folder (only a system service, not a process of the game), is not detected; that is why the setting stays off, expert only and with a warning. A harmless name that happens to match only prevents the freeze. A parent folder of the install folder that is a symbolic link to a network share is not detected as a share; the bounds of the scan (2 seconds, 50,000 names) still apply, and the result can be "unknown" (then nothing is frozen).

### PlayStation controllers (HID)

Threat model since 0.5.0 (DualShock 4 and DualSense without Steam or DS4Windows).
- **Access:** Only Sony pads (vendor 054C, known product ids) are opened; other HID devices (keyboard, mouse, touchpad) are not even opened for a probe, because the device path must already name the vendor id, and a Sony device that is no pad is probed once. Pads are opened, read-only (`GENERIC_READ`) and shared (`FILE_SHARE_READ | FILE_SHARE_WRITE`): games, Steam and DS4Windows keep reading. No driver, no administrator rights, no exclusive access. A pad that another program holds exclusively is skipped (once in the log).
- **Nothing is written:** no feature reports, no output reports, no rumble, no light bar. So CouchHUD does not switch a Bluetooth pad into its full mode either; the battery level is missing until another program does.
- **Parsing:** reads go into a fixed buffer of the size the device reports (at most 1024 bytes, larger devices are not opened). The parser checks the length, the report id and the transport, reads fixed positions only and returns nothing for anything else; broken reports cause no crash and no allocation that depends on their content (fuzz tests). At most four PlayStation pads.
- **What is evaluated:** buttons, sticks, triggers and the battery level. Gyro, acceleration, touchpad coordinates and the serial number are not read; the touchpad click is just a button. None of it goes to the log, the page, the phone, MCP, plugins or the assistant; the page only learns "xbox" or "playstation" as the kind of controller.
- **Counting:** a pad and its Steam Input or DS4Windows mirror count once (recognized when at least 6 of the last 8 presses were the same on both within 80 ms; a pad of its own only after presses in a row alone (6, for a known mirror 8) while the PlayStation pad holds nothing, plus at least 4 of 8 PlayStation presses it does not answer; no such presses count shortly after a PlayStation pad went away or while one cannot be read; so activators like long press or turbo never turn a mirror into a second controller; Steam's virtual pad next to a PlayStation pad never counts as a second controller). Until that is known, CouchHUD takes the smaller number: at worst Bluetooth does not stay off (it comes back by itself), instead of leaving the couch without its only controller. The DS4 wireless adapter without a pad (empty reports) is no controller; with no report for half a second everything counts as released. When reads keep failing while the device stays listed, CouchHUD reopens it only after 2, 4, 8 … at most 60 seconds.
- **Lock:** PS is the same logical button as Guide and goes through the same checks (couch lock, parental controls, screenshot rules). There is no new way in and no new operation to the service.
- Known limits: whoever runs as the same user can read the pad anyway. Games see every press too (as with XInput). A Steam Input layout that remaps buttons only affects the mirrored Xbox pad, not the original.

### TV (LG webOS)

TV control (since 0.5) only talks to the TV inside the home network. Deliberately:
- Only private or link-local addresses, never this PC's own address (10/8, 172.16/12, 192.168/16, 169.254/16, fd00::/8); public addresses, loopback, host names and network or broadcast addresses (of the PC's matching subnet, otherwise x.x.x.0 and x.x.x.255) are refused, also in search answers. No redirects, no proxy, messages at most 256 KB, fixed timeouts, at most one reconnect per command; after a failure no new connection for 2 seconds (whoever asks), automatic reads only after 15 seconds.
- The TV's certificate is self-signed. CouchHUD pins the fingerprint of its public key at pairing (trust on first use) and afterwards talks to exactly that one; a different certificate means "Pair TV again". The check applies to this connection only, never globally. Someone in the network during the **first** pairing can pose as the TV. A pairing without a key only counts when the TV really asked first (otherwise "unexpected answer"). Pairing again after "different certificate" says clearly, before and after, that it is a different certificate than before.
- **Unencrypted (port 3000)** only for older TVs without wss (port 3001): only when 3001 does not answer at all (a TLS failure on 3001, such as a refused handshake or a reset connection, shuts the plain port for the rest of that pairing attempt), only in expert mode after an explicit question, and never for an address or MAC that was ever paired with encryption (CouchHUD remembers those, also after "Remove TV"; at most 32). This only holds while the TV's IP address and MAC stay the same and are not forged: if the TV gets a new address by DHCP and someone in the network also spoofs the ARP answer (MAC), it does not help; then only the question in expert mode remains. If this PC already had an encrypted pairing, the question says so clearly ("only without encryption if you are sure this is an old TV"). The page then shows "unencrypted". **Limit:** with an unencrypted pairing the pairing key and every command cross the home network in clear text; anyone listening can control the TV afterwards (volume, input, off). That is a property of the old protocol and cannot be fixed in CouchHUD.
- The pairing key is stored DPAPI-encrypted (current user) in `config.json`, never in the log or the UI. A program running as the same user can decrypt it.
- Wake-on-LAN only goes to the paired TV's MAC, only as a broadcast into the TV's subnet (TTL 1), at most every 10 seconds.
- While the couch is locked, only on/off, volume and mute work; search, pairing, removing, changing the MAC, reading and switching inputs need the unlock. Besides the bridge, the TV service in the host checks this itself. With parental controls switching inputs also needs the parent code, bound to exactly the chosen input (the code dialog names it; an invalid input id spends no release). Deliberate order: before the release the host only checks the id's form; whether the TV knows the input is checked afterwards against a fresh list. A valid-looking but unknown id therefore spends the release without switching, and a new code is needed (spending the release first keeps it from lying around for another attempt). The phone remote and MCP/assistant have no access.
- Sleep and shutdown from CouchHUD wait at most 2.5 seconds for the TV.
- What other devices in the home network do with the TV (every paired device can control it) is outside CouchHUD.

### Phone remote: power (from 0.7.0)

The paired phone can put the PC to sleep, restart it and shut it down. Threat model: the phone page is a network surface (plain HTTP on the home network). Attackers: a device on the Wi-Fi that is not paired, a foreign web page in the phone's browser (CSRF), whoever records a request and sends it again, a child with a paired phone who wants past the lock or parental controls, and a stray tap.
- **No new way in:** the phone ends in the same host call as the entries of the power menu on the TV (the bridge's `power`, `Hud.PowerAsync`), with no operation of its own and no call to the service. A fixed list `shutdown`, `restart`, `sleep`, spelled exactly; everything else (also "restart into PC mode", sign out, quit) is refused.
- **The same rules as on the TV:** `RemotePower.Decide` checks with the same lists the bridge uses for the power call (waiting profile picker, couch lock, switchable areas) and also whether the entry asks for the code on the TV. Today standby, restart and shut down do not ask for the code on the TV either; should an entry do so (a contract test keeps the list equal to `ui/core/power.js`), the phone is refused on the locked couch, because it never takes a code over the unencrypted connection. Parental controls do not limit power on the TV, so they do not on the phone. Checked when asking and again right before running.
- **Not in desktop mode:** when CouchHUD is in PC mode, someone works at the desktop; the phone is refused then ("The PC is in desktop mode – switch it off at the PC"), so nobody switches a running desktop away from someone else.
- **Can be turned off:** Settings › Phone › "Phone may shut down / restart the PC" (default: on). Like every setting it is behind the couch lock, and the PC checks it on every request (`RemotePower.Decide`, before all other rules); when it is off the phone page hides the section and the PC refuses every request.
- **Paired devices only, home network only:** the same pairing (device token in the `X-CouchHud-Token` header, stored only as a hash) and the same address rule as the other endpoints, plus the same origin check (`Origin`); the custom header forces a preflight for foreign pages, which the server does not answer.
- **Two steps, once:** `/api/power/prepare` hands out a random ticket after the checks, only `/api/power` with that ticket switches. The ticket counts for the same phone and the same action, no sooner than 0.8 and no later than 30 seconds, exactly once; every try spends it, a new question replaces the old one. A recorded request cannot be sent again, a double tap never confirms. The page shows a question with "Cancel" on top and the action somewhere else, usable only after a second, and names a running game.
- **Rate limit:** at most 10 requests a minute per phone, both steps together; counted right after the pairing check, so refused and unknown requests count too, and a throttled confirmation still spends its ticket. After an action ran, none for 20 seconds, from any phone (with its own message "A power command was just given"); when the action did not run (refused, or Windows said no), the pause it started is lifted (never another phone's). Requests to the phone server are capped at 32 KiB, pairing included.
- **Log and messages:** one line per try with the action, the phone's short id and name and the outcome, never the token or the ticket. The name is cleaned the same way for the log and the TV (`RemotePower.CleanName`: no control or format characters such as bidi overrides or invisible characters, at most 40 characters). The TV briefly shows which phone did what, only once Windows took the command. When the command fails the phone gets a fixed text; Windows' error message goes to the log only.
- **Limits:** whoever reads along on the Wi-Fi sees the device token (as for all phone functions) and could fetch a ticket with it; that is the same limit as for buttons and text from the phone, and unpairing revokes it. A child with a paired phone can switch the PC off, as it can with the controller. From 0.7 the sleep timer can be set from the phone too; it ends in the same power call and needs the same clearance (see the section on the encrypted channel); a running timer, set at the TV or on a phone, ends when CouchHUD enters PC mode.

### Phone remote: encrypted channel and library (from 0.7)

Phones paired from 0.7 on talk over an encrypted channel; the page stays HTTP on the home network because a phone browser does not trust a self-signed certificate without installing it. Threat model: devices on the Wi-Fi that are not paired, whoever listens in (also a stolen or recorded token), foreign web pages in the phone's browser (CSRF, DNS rebinding), replay of recorded requests, game names with markup (XSS), overload, a child with a paired phone.
- **Key from the QR code:** the QR link `http://<ip>:<port>/#k=<32 bytes>` carries the secret in the fragment, which the browser never sends; the page removes it from the address bar at once. Pairing (`/api/pair`) is sealed with a key derived from it, the answer brings the device id and the device key (32 bytes, protected with DPAPI on the PC). The secret is valid for 10 minutes and for exactly one phone. There is no plain-text pairing any more.
- **Sealing "seal v1"** (`RemoteSeal`, `ui/remote/seal.js`, byte-identical on both sides by a test vector): HMAC-SHA256 as a key stream (CTR) with encrypt-then-MAC over direction, number, context (session and purpose), nonce and ciphertext; without WebCrypto, because `crypto.subtle` is missing on HTTP. Every session (`/api/session`) has its own key from the device key and two fresh nonces; requests carry a number the PC accepts only once (window of 64), the answer carries the same number and another direction, so an answer cannot be played back as a request. Sessions end after 30 minutes unused, after 24 hours at the latest, at most 4 per phone.
- **Events and covers:** `/api/events` (server-sent events) and `/art/<hash>` need a proof in the URL (`UrlProof` with the session key), for events with a rising number per session; events are sealed and only name what changed. Covers need the *Library* level.
- **Rights per phone:** *Control only* (state, buttons, text, power, sleep timer), *Library* (plus library, collections, downloads, start, favourite, parents' code), *Full* (plus rename, hide/show, edit collections, reload artwork and a small fixed list of settings, see below). New phones get *Control only*; changing it only on the TV with the parents' code. A phone paired in a child profile has at most *Library* while that profile is active, otherwise *Control only*. Every operation then goes through the same bridge as on the TV (`UiBridge.InvokeAsync`: waiting profile picker, couch lock, profile, areas, parental controls, function levels), so a phone never has more than the couch has right now. **PC mode:** couch lock and parental controls are not enforced there (`IsUnlocked`, `ParentalEnforced`); so that the phone cannot start, rename, hide or change collections past them, it is only *Control only* in PC mode (`RemoteRights.Effective` with `pcMode`, in one place; channel and host both check it, answer code "pcMode"). The state reports the lowered level and the page explains it. Power stays refused there as before. A sleep timer ends when PC mode is entered (set at the TV or on a phone) and can neither start nor run out there (power.js checks again when it runs out). **Sleep timer:** off needs the same check as on (right, couch lock, the "phone may switch off" setting); a child's phone ends only a timer a child's phone set, never a parent's or the TV's, and it does not replace a running foreign timer with a new one either (same rule, `MayEndSleepTimer`). The PC knows which timer came from a phone by a random token that goes to power.js with the start and returns with the report of that timer (no time window that a timer set at the TV could hit).
- **Little data to the phone:** library, downloads and collections go to the phone as a fixed selection of single fields (`RemoteCalls.Entry`, `RemoteCalls.Project`), never as internal records (no path, no error detail, no plugin text). The name of a running game is in the state only from *Library* on, and only when the game would be visible in that phone's library; the same holds for the question before switching off (`powerPrepare`): without that right the PC only says that a game is running, without a name. There is no "session" call for phones any more (the TV's record would carry the name unfiltered). Collections are cut to the entries of the phone's visible library (in the answers to collection changes too); a collection that has entries of which the phone sees none is left out whole, since its name alone would tell what is hidden.
- **Parents' code on the phone:** only over the channel, only when the couch lock is the CouchHUD code (never the Windows password) and the phone is not a child's phone, only within 2 minutes after the PC asked for it for exactly this action and entry, through the existing grant (with its lockout after wrong tries: wrong tries from the phone count towards the PC's lockout, not only the phone's own) and in addition at most 5 tries in 10 minutes per phone. The code never appears in the log. Otherwise the PC refuses: "confirm on the TV".
- **Settings from the phone (Full, from 0.7):** Threat model: a child or a guest with a phone that has Full; whoever hijacks a Full phone (a stolen device, a key from the Wi-Fi traffic, see limits); a foreign web page or a device on the Wi-Fi trying to inject values; replay of recorded requests; a phone trying to give itself more rights. Rules:
  - **A fixed positive list on the PC** (`RemoteSettings.All`): `language` (auto, de, en), `favoritesMode` (favorites, recent, combined), `favoritesRecentDays` (7, 30, 90, 365, 0), `favoritesSites`, `screensaverMinutes` (0, 2, 5, 10, 15, 30, as the TV's picker), `screensaverStyle` (art, clock, dim, black), `audioMode` (off, ui, music), `soundVolume` and `musicVolume` (whole number 0 to 100), and the skin (its own call `skin`, only an id from the PC's own skin list). Every entry has a type and a range: the value must have exactly that type (no "5" for 5, no 1.5, no text for a switch) and be in the list or the range, case counts. Anything else the PC refuses before the bridge sees it (`RemoteSettings.Map`), even if the phone page would never send it; the call to the bridge is rebuilt from the checked value, extra fields do not get through. The phone page shows only what the PC names in `schema`.
  - **Never from the phone (not on the list, so refused):** couch lock and code, parents' code, parental controls, child profiles, shell, sign-in and automatic sign-in, services, every privacy switch (offline master switch, network services, update checks, online artwork), mirroring Windows notifications (`mirrorNotifications`: it shows content of other programs to everyone in the room, and Windows asks for permission where nobody sees it), plugins, their rights, installation and catalog sources, MCP and assistant with their keys, switchable areas, expert mode, mouse mode, start and return rules, and the phones' own rights. A phone therefore cannot raise itself: there is neither a call nor a key for it, and the level lives in the pairing record, which only the TV changes, with the parents' code. A test pins the list and checks a long series of dangerous keys with any values.
  - **The same path as on the TV:** values go through the same bridge (`setSetting` and `setSkin`) and so through the same setters as the settings page: waiting profile picker, couch lock (locked, only the settings that are harmless for a locked guest work, the rest says "unlock at the TV"), switchable areas, then the setter with its checks and side effects. A skin must be in the PC's skin list (`SkinCatalog.IsKnown`); the id is never used as a path and has a checked shape only (`default` or `user:` plus a folder name without separators, colon, `..` or control characters, at most 100 characters).
  - **The parents' code where the TV asks for it:** with parental controls and several profiles the screensaver is a parents' setting (see "Back to the picker"). The phone is then asked for exactly the same grant (action `idleSetting`, entry `screensaverMinutes`) and answers it over the channel's one existing parents' code path (`parentCode`: only encrypted, only after the PC asked for exactly this action and entry, 5 tries per 10 minutes, never a child's phone, only with the CouchHUD code); the setter uses the grant up as on the TV. There is no second code path. With the Windows password as couch lock one confirms at the TV.
  - **Right and mode:** Full only. In PC mode every phone is control only (answer "pcMode"), a child's phone is capped at Library, a phone without a level has Control; the check is `RemoteRights.Effective`, run in the channel and once more on the host's UI thread. The event kind `settings` goes to Full phones only.
  - **Replay and overload:** every request is sealed and numbered like all others (a recorded call is accepted once), and a value only sets itself: the same request twice gives the same result. The per-session limit (240 requests a minute) and the per-source limit apply.
  - **Live both ways:** when the phone changes something the TV page gets the event `settings` or `skin` (volume, screensaver and skin at once; for a new language the page reloads, at most once in ten seconds and only when no code entry, dialog or profile picker is open; otherwise the reload waits and is made up afterwards); when the TV (or a tool) changes a setting of the list, the setters tell the Full phones, which fetch their values again. The phone gets only the values of the list, checked like a value it could set (`RemoteSettings.Project`): never the host's record, no keys, no paths, no style sheets or sounds of the skins (id and name only).
  - **What a hijacked Full phone can do at most:** change the look, the language, the sounds, the Favourites tab and the screensaver (with parental controls only with the parents' code, which it does not know), besides what Full may do anyway (rename and hide in the library, collections, start games). It cannot lower any protection, install or start anything that is not already in the library, or grant rights.
- **Web protection:** the server answers only when the `Host` header is an IP address (against DNS rebinding, else 421), still checks `Origin` and the home network, sends `nosniff`, `no-referrer`, `no-store` and for the page a CSP without foreign sources (`connect-src 'self'`, `frame-ancestors 'none'`). The page sets library names as text only. Every request is capped at 32 KiB; at most 240 requests a minute per session, sessions limited in total. A request that names a phone but fails the seal (or an unknown phone) renews nothing (the 180 days start again only after the seal opened) and counts as a wrong try per source address: after 5 the address rests for 30 seconds (as with pairing). The table of addresses is hard-capped (256): when it is full the oldest entry makes room (one that is not resting first); it is never emptied, so a flood of new addresses lifts no pause. IPv6 addresses count by their /64 prefix, IPv4-mapped ones (`::ffff:a.b.c.d`) as IPv4; expired pauses are swept at most once a second. Loopback never rests: only a process of this PC (which reaches the key anyway) or a proxy on the PC comes from there, where one pause would lock out every phone. The count of wrong tries is cleared only after a request that opened and was admitted (not by a replayed one). Pairing (QR code) uses the same brake with its own table: cap, /64, loopback and sweeping are the same there, and a new QR code does not lift a pause.
- **Limits:** without HTTPS, an active attacker on the Wi-Fi (who redirects traffic, not just listens) can hand the phone a changed page while it loads; the channel protects against passive listening, replay and foreign pages, but not against this attacker. In detail:
  - A **parents' code typed on the phone** can be intercepted that way. It stays valid, so all of parental controls fall, not just the one action.
  - The **QR secret** can be taken while scanning and loading; whoever has it within the 10 minutes can pair themselves.
  - The **device key** is in the phone browser's `localStorage`, without forward secrecy, and valid for up to 180 days from last use; whoever has it once can also read recorded traffic of that phone later.
  - **Metadata** (length and time of requests, which address) is visible to anyone listening, the content is not.
  - The **QR secret is on the TV for 10 minutes** and can end up in screenshots, streams and recordings. Afterwards it is worthless.
  - The **CSP with `'unsafe-inline'`** is not a layer of protection: it keeps foreign sources out but does not stop injected inline code and does not protect against a swapped page.
  - **Recommendation:** when in doubt, type the parents' code at the TV (where it cannot be intercepted) and do not open the phone page on foreign Wi-Fi. Whoever wants no parents' actions on the phone sets the couch lock to the Windows password; the phone takes no code then. Whoever holds the phone has its rights; unpairing on the TV revokes them at once. Phones paired before 0.7 keep talking over the old connection with a token (control and power only, see above) until they are paired again.

### Second confirmation on the phone (from 0.7, optional, off by default)

If you want, critical things need a yes on a paired phone after the code (*Settings › Phone › Second confirmation on the phone*). It is an extra safeguard against someone at the couch who knows the code or watched it being typed; it promises no more than that.

- **Flow:** First the code at the controller (couch or parents' code, as before), then the TV shows a two-digit number and the phone shows the question with the action, a short line (profile, rule, plugin), the time and a keypad: the phone is never told the number and offers no choice, the number is read on the TV and typed. A wrong number ends the request at once and locks this phone: 1 minute after the first failed try, 10 minutes after the second, 1 hour from the third on; a right confirmation starts that phone's count over. The lock is per phone, so a foreign phone cannot lock the parents' phone out; across all phones together 10 failed tries within an hour shut the phone way for everybody for an hour. Then only a Windows administrator works (which resets the count). A failed try stamped in the future when loading (clock turned back or file edited) still counts, capped to now. The count lives in a small file (`second-factor-state.json` next to the settings) and survives a restart; if the file and its copy cannot be read, the phone way is shut for an hour (fail closed). Limit: a process of the same user can delete the file, as it can edit `config.json`; the service's state would keep it better (0.7.x). New questions come at most every 10 seconds. The request is valid for 60 seconds, once, and bound to exactly this action with its parameters (SHA-256 over them), a random request id and the confirming phone: if that phone is unpaired, lowered or no longer allowed to confirm before the action runs, the ticket is void. A profile switch, unpairing or lowering a phone and PC mode discard open questions and tickets. The answer travels over the encrypted channel. B on the TV cancels.
- **Requirements:** The code check (couch lock, code or Windows sign-in) is on, and there is a paired phone with the right *Full* that is not a child's phone; otherwise it cannot be switched on. Only such phones may confirm (not while a child's profile is on the couch or PC mode is on, either). If the requirements go (code check off, last phone unpaired or lowered), the confirmation switches itself off with a notice. Whoever switches the code check off gets a clear warning; with the second confirmation on, an even clearer one because it goes with it.
- **Critical actions** (a positive list in one place, `SecondFactorActions`; reasons per entry there): set up automatic sign-in, switch the shell on/off, change or switch off the couch lock (also a new code under the same mode), new controller code, new parents' code (also in the chain right after switching parental controls on, which skips only the parents' code step, never this confirmation), parental controls off, change profile rules (the direction is not told apart: tightening needs it too), switch from a child's profile to one without limits, PC mode (also the restart into it and quitting CouchHUD when that brings up the desktop; the switch to light mode puts shell, lock and Task Manager block into one question), switching the second confirmation itself off, giving a phone more rights (lowering needs nothing), pairing a new phone (on top of the code step at the TV: the QR secret appears and works only after a phone that is paired already confirmed, for one phone), releasing a plugin's rights (function level, approval, allowing a withdrawn plugin, confirming a program) and everything that makes installing approving at the same time: a local package (bound to the file's name and hash), a package from another developer's catalog, an update with new capabilities (bound to the entry and the hash), a new catalog and switching the automatic plugin update on; CouchHUD's own signed packages without new capabilities and skins stay free. Also: switching Offline off, letting the Task Manager through. Deliberately free (the parents' code covers them, a known limit): adding a game, changing start options and the type of an entry. Also free, each behind the parents' code for exactly that action: locking or unlocking a single game (`setParentalItemLock`: one entry, no rule set), resetting the day's counter (`parentalReset`: as everyday as extending play time), accepting the clock after a detected time change (`parentalAcceptClock`) and adopting the PC's time zone for the profile (`parentalAdoptZone`); the last two switch no limit off, they keep the timekeeping in step with the PC. To add them, put them on the positive list (`SecondFactorActions`, an entry and a guard). Every action that asks for a code is either there or in the exemption list with a reason (everyday things like extending play time or starting a locked game, and everything that only raises protection); the same goes for every service operation that takes a grant. Tests make sure nothing stays unclassified and every action on the list has a guard in the program. On top of that a test reads every body of the program that writes protection state with Roslyn (lock mode, codes, parental controls and profile rules, Task Manager block, shell entry, PC mode, pairing, plugin trust, the service's codes and mode, automatic sign-in) and demands a call of the guard before it as a statement of the body (also inside if and switch, not in a lambda, a local function, a loop or a try) or a reasoned exemption; for “the callers guard” it checks every caller, method groups like `Task.Run(X)` included. **Limits of this check** (a net, not a proof): it cannot see whether the condition of an `if` around the guard is right, nor whether a caller lets the exception (`SecondFactorNeededException`) pass, and it knows only the effects on its list; a new way of writing under another name is not noticed until it is put on the list.
- **Fixes from the security review:** (1) *Switching a plugin back on* needs the second confirmation (plugin rights) when it is not CouchHUD's own or may use the network, the assistant or programs; *switching off* stays free, so the parents' code alone cannot undo a lock made by disabling. (2) A companion rule that newly starts on the locked couch (`WhenLocked`) or beside every game (mode "all") needs it (its own action `companionLocked`); plain rules and removing a rule stay free. (3) Uninstalling a plugin no longer deletes the parents' function levels (they stay under the plugin id) and they are no longer tidied at start either: a reinstall from the official catalog needs no second confirmation and must not reset a function lock to the defaults. Removing levels is on the effect test's (Roslyn) list. (4) The PC mode tray icon is disposed at once on the UI thread when leaving, not queued (entering PC mode again could otherwise remove the new icon).
- **Known limits (review):** (a) Function levels stay under the plugin id forever. A third-party plugin that later reuses the same id inherits them, so a "restricted" level carries over. This is mitigated because installing a third-party plugin needs the second confirmation anyway; binding the levels to origin or signature is planned for 0.7.x. (b) The "games" mode of a companion rule with a long game list needs no second confirmation. (c) Editing the config file by hand bypasses both the companion and the enable guard, as for every protection that rests on the configuration.
- **Where it is enforced (honestly):** In CouchHUD itself, where the action is carried out (`Hud.RequireSecondFactor`), after the code check and before the grant is used up; the UI cannot skip it because the program, not the page, checks the phone's answer and hands out a one-time ticket for exactly that action. The **service** (SYSTEM) **cannot** verify the confirmation: the channel to the phone ends in CouchHUD's process, the device key lives in the user's profile, and the service has no secret that only the phone holds. A message “the phone confirmed” to the service could be sent just as well by any process of the same user. The limit: **a process of the same user** that talks to the service pipe itself or edits `config.json` (it says whether the feature is on; keeping it in the service's state like the lock mode would be the improvement for 0.7.x) bypasses the second confirmation; it could ask for the grants itself anyway if it knows the code. This protects against the person at the couch, not against malware in the account. On the service side the grants (code or administrator) are unchanged.
- **Emergency way:** If the phone is lost or empty, a Windows administrator does it (UAC through the same path as an administrator's approval, on the secure desktop); the TV offers it in the question. That is deliberately no bypass: an administrator can do everything anyway. **But:** on an account that is an administrator itself (Admin Approval Mode) UAC is only a yes/no click without credentials; there the emergency way is no real second proof, and whoever holds the controller gets through with one click. When switching on, CouchHUD checks the token (elevation type) and warns clearly (“This Windows account is an administrator – the emergency way is then only a click. For real protection use a standard account.”). A password prompt of its own instead of UAC is not built; that is Kevin's decision and is on the list. With no phone and no administrator nothing happens (fail closed). A restart discards open questions and tickets (memory only).

**Threat model.**

  - *Blind confirming* (someone taps “yes” on the phone without reading): there is no “yes” and no choice: the number has to be read on the TV and typed. Blind guessing hits with 1/90 per try, and every failed try locks (1 minute, 10 minutes, then 1 hour). The number protects against slips and against someone who only has the phone, not against a child who sees TV and phone and does not mistype.
  - *Malicious or stolen phone* with the right *Full*: can confirm if it sees the TV's number (so it must be in front of the TV) and the code at the controller was right before. Counter: unpair the phone at the TV (immediate, also for running questions); a phone outside the room does not learn the number. Whoever has the phone unlocked and is in the room has its rights.
  - *Replay:* a recorded channel call is rejected by nonce/counter (channel); a request id counts for exactly one answer (right or wrong), the ticket is used up at the first execution and expires after 60 s.
  - *Race between two actions:* one question is open at a time; a second action replaces the first, whose number is dead at once. The ticket is for action + parameter hash only: a confirmation for “lock off” opens nothing else and not “lock to Windows”.
  - *The child knows the code:* exactly what the feature is for; without a phone with *Full* (not theirs) they get no further. If they paired a phone, it is a child profile phone (at most *Library*, never confirms).
  - *The child has a paired phone:* as above; a phone paired in a child profile does not confirm, not even after a profile switch. If the child pairs a new one itself, that needs the confirmation of a parent's phone.
  - *Downgrade* (switching the confirmation off without having it): switching off is itself a critical action (code and phone). The code check does not get around it either: changing the couch lock needs it as well. The same goes for the last phone that can confirm: unpairing or lowering it ends the feature by itself and therefore needs it too.
  - *Emergency way as a bypass:* on a standard account the administrator needs UAC with Windows credentials the child does not have; if they have them, everything is open anyway. On an administrator account it is only a click (see above, with a warning when switching on). It changes nothing about the rest of the rights.
  - *Restart in the middle of the question:* questions and tickets exist only in memory; after a restart there is nothing to confirm and the action starts again with the code.
  - *Attacker in the LAN:* does not see the question (encrypted channel, device key needed), cannot answer or trigger it. They can only try to pair: that needs the QR secret, which with the second confirmation on appears only after a phone confirmed. Active attackers on the Wi-Fi are otherwise as in the phone remote section.

### Web sign-in of a plugin (`browser.signin`)

A sign-in on a web page (in CouchHUD mainly Home Assistant's, in the "Home Assistant" plugin) runs in the built-in browser; neither CouchHUD nor the plugin sees user name and password. The program has no sign-in of its own for Home Assistant any more (since 0.6, deleted); it only offers plugins with the capability `browser.signin` (CouchHUD's own plugins only) this one general route. Exchanging the code, PKCE, refreshing and revoking the access are the plugin's business (it binds every access to the address it was made for); the program takes care of this:

- **No port, the redirect is never loaded:** the redirect address has a host ending in `.invalid` (RFC 6761: never resolves), named by the plugin and matching a fixed pattern. The browser cancels every navigation to that host in `NavigationStarting` before a request goes out and hands the address to the program once. In frames it is only cancelled and never taken. As a second layer the browser answers every request to any `.invalid` host that gets past the navigation checks (an image, `fetch`, a form in a frame, a shared or service worker) itself with 403 through a `WebResourceRequested` filter; it never goes out. The host stays cancelled until the browser closes, also after the sign-in ended or ran out. No loopback listener on purpose: an open port on 127.0.0.1 would be reachable for every local process.
- **Only its own flow:** the sign-in page must carry exactly one `state` of URL-safe characters (at most 256) in its address; without it the sign-in does not even start. A redirect is only taken with exactly this `state` (compared in constant time), once, and only when it comes from a page of the same origin (scheme, host, port) as the sign-in page. Everything else (no flow, another or broken state) is ignored and leaves the page open; a foreign page can neither end the sign-in nor close the browser. Only one sign-in runs at a time; another plugin's is not taken over.
- **A child on the couch:** the sign-in only opens with the couch unlocked and the Browser area on; when the redirect is taken the program checks the lock once more before it hands the address to the plugin. Opening the sign-in page does not extend an unlock (it does not count as use of the browser, only real input does; contract test `BrowserUnlockContractTests`). If the browser closes (unlock ran out, close button), the sign-in ends too. When the browser opens it says which plugin opened it.
- **Code and state stay invisible:** the redirect is never loaded, so it is never in the address bar, the history or the recent sites. As long as the sign-in page is open (also after it ran out), the address bar shows its pages without the query (so without the state) and keeps them out of the recent sites. Every answer of the sign-in closes the browser with the sign-in page at once; with the WebView2 window its back list is gone too. **Limit:** Chromium's browser profile on disk may keep the sign-in page's address with the state in its history; CouchHUD shows that history nowhere, and the state is used up or expired by then. Address, code, state and token are never in a log.

### Companion apps, apps in the Guide menu, media players (from 0.7)

A companion app starts with a game, an entry in the Guide menu starts an app during the game. Neither may be a way around the couch lock or parental controls.

- **Library only, never a path:** A rule names a library app by its id only (no path, no parameter, no program name; a test pins the fields). What runs is the start the library decides on at that moment (the user's own start target and parameters included). A hand-edited `config.json` is cleaned when read (at most 12 rules and 50 games per rule, an unknown mode means "chosen games", never "every game").
- **Only a plain program, on its real path:** No script file (`.bat`, `.cmd`), no console or scripting language, no Windows tool, no browser, no game client, nothing from the Windows folder, no network path (`\\server`, `\\?\`), no web page, protocol or Store app. This is the check that lets an entry be a game on the locked couch (`GameEntryRules.MayBeGame`). It does not run on the spelling in the library but on the **real path**: the file is opened (nobody may write or delete it meanwhile), Windows names the final path from the open handle (links, junctions and 8.3 names resolved, `GetFinalPathNameByHandle` and `GetLongPathName`), and the name and folder rules run on that. A path with a data stream (`a.txt:x.exe`), a wildcard, a `..` or `.` part, or a trailing dot or space is never opened; a link to a program with another file name is refused. The check runs when the rule is made, before every start, and once more on the held file, and CreateProcess gets exactly that path. A companion app cannot be a chain to a locked program. **Limit of the name filter:** a program under a different name is not recognised (a copy of `cmd.exe` called `mytool.exe` passes the name filter); whoever adds one needs the unlock and, with parental controls, the parents' code.
- **The same locks as a start from the library:** Area off means nothing starts; a protected profile still waiting for its code, or an unusable `config.json`, means nothing starts; on a locked couch nothing starts unless the rule says so explicitly (and the program passes the check above) or the couch was open when the game was started (the game start forgets the unlock at once, so the intent is noted once for that game). In a child's profile only what the child could start without the parents' code starts (list, age rating, time rules); a start that would ask for the code or only advise does not happen. It is decided twice: when the game starts and again after waiting for the foreground, never from an old result.
- **Rights:** Rules are changed only through CouchHUD's own page, unlocked, with a chosen profile and, with parental controls, with a fresh parents' code that is used up only after all checks passed. No remote, MCP, assistant or plugin has a way to it (test). Reading the Guide list is open but answers nothing while locked.
- **Ending foreign processes:** Only what CouchHUD started itself is ended, only with the option on (off by default) and only while it is the same program file with the same start time. CouchHUD reads the new process's start time and program file from the process handle CreateProcess returns, before it is closed (not afterwards by process id); if the program file is not the one that was checked, that process is never ended. When ending, the Windows call checks the start time **and** the program file on the very handle that ends (no gap between checking and ending), after a polite close with a grace period. **The processes the companion app started itself are ended with it**, recognized by parent process id plus start time (a child is never older than its parent). Without a known start time nothing is ever ended. Windows, CouchHUD itself, other sessions and the game's processes are excluded (`SafeTermination`). If a launcher runs briefly and hands over to another process, that process is left alone.
- **Focus:** A companion app starts only once the game is in front, without `AllowSetForegroundWindow` and without activating its window. Whatever moves to the front anyway is only minimized, and only the companion app itself and its process tree (process id plus descendants with a start-time check), never "everything new". Never touched: Windows' own programs, anything in the Windows folder, standard dialogs (class `#32770`), other sessions, other integrity levels, the game and CouchHUD. A program that asks for administrator rights does not start (no UAC prompt over the game).
- **Limit:** Between the check and the start someone with write access to a folder on the path can replace it with a junction (the file itself is held). Whoever has write access to program folders or `config.json` is outside the protection (see above).
- **Wand (from 0.7):** "Set up Wand" appears only if `Wand.exe` is at a usual place **and** its signature holds. This is checked on the held file: WinVerifyTrust gets the open handle (no network lookup) and CouchHUD reads the signer from WinVerifyTrust's verification state (not from the file again). CouchHUD then builds the chain itself with `CustomRootTrust` and only the machine roots (`LocalMachine\Root` and `LocalMachine\AuthRoot`; a root added by the user does not count), requires the Code Signing usage (1.3.6.1.5.5.7.3.3), with no revocation lookup; if the chain does not hold, the file counts as not verified. The publisher (field O or CN, exactly equal, ignoring case) must be on a small list (`WandSetup.KnownPublishers`, no substring, no thumbprint pin because the certificate rotates). The rule keeps the SHA-256 of the checked file. **At every start** the hash of the held file is computed: the same means start; a different one (Wand updated itself) means the signature check runs again on the held file, and if it holds the new hash is kept, otherwise nothing starts, the page shows "Signature invalid" and a notification appears. Without a valid signature there is at most "Add as an app" with an "unverified program" warning (no companion rule, no Guide menu). Every confirmation shows the full path. The one-time hint appears, and counts as shown, only once the couch is unlocked.
- **Accepted residual risk, no revocation check:** The signature check runs without the network (no CRL and no OCSP lookup), so nothing goes out and the start works offline. A WeMod certificate revoked later would therefore still be accepted until it expires or the root is removed. This is decided and recorded here.
- **A damaged hash is not "no hash":** If a rule holds a hash field that is not a SHA-256 (a hand-edited `config.json`), the rule stays marked "signature invalid": it does not start and does not become an ordinary companion app. Only a rule that never had a hash is an ordinary one.
- **Working folder and parameters are not path-checked:** The check is of the program (the real path of the file). An app's working folder and parameters are those of its library entry (startup options, changed only unlocked in expert mode and with the parents' code) and are not checked as paths again for a companion app.
- **Guide menu and parental controls:** With parental controls on, an app from the Guide menu does not start while a game is running (`closeGameFirst`, as in the library). This is intended: it is the same start.
- **Decided anew before every start:** Before each single companion app, the area, the profile, the child rules, the couch lock and the program check are asked again; if one says no, the rest of the round does not start either. The intent "the couch was open when the game started" is noted only after the game's start succeeded.
- **Locked couch: confirmed program (from 0.7.1):** A companion app with "also when locked" starts past the child's own list. There it only starts when the parent confirmed exactly this program: a digest (format version `3:`; older pins count as not confirmed) over the real path of the held file, its parameters, its working folder, the file's SHA-256 and the choice about a writable folder.
